—Idle Hands Find Pleasant Task New Bedford Man Has Been 
at Hospital in 


England. 


Writes That American Troops 
Are Making a Big Hit 
Abroad, 


Word of another New Bedford man 
in a hospital in England reached here 
today when a letter from Regimental 
Supply Sergeant Charles BE, Chapin 
formerly of the fourth company, C. A. 
Cx was received, stating that he had 
just been discharged after having been 
confined two weeks, eight days of 
which period he spent in bed. 
Sergeant Chapin was suffering from 
an infected hand, but is now dis-] 
charged, and presumably at the pres- 
ent time is in France, for he stated in 
his letter that he had been placed in| 
command of a detachment of Ameri- 


Ses us 


Left to Hight—Blsie Dufrane, Mary Lisbon, Rose Benjamin, Alexandra April, Ida Francis, Evelyn Mello. 
In Background—Nellic Flores, Vive Worgren, Brinadette Soulle. 


CORNER YOUNG MEN 


Sergeant Charles E. Chapin, 


ean soldiers recently discharged from 
London hospitals and was to. take 
them to the American base in Franee 
the next day. 

Sergeant Chapin went out of here 
with the fourth company in July, was 
transferred to the 101st ammunition 
train at Westfield, and on. October 2) 
sailed for France, His: letter of to-! 
day is the first that his family has re- 
ceived from him since his departure} 
from America. 

He says that the American troops 
fare making a big hit abroad and that 
they are referred to as the “American 
Shell Eaters,’ because in the few ae- 
tions in which they have participated 
they have shown a ‘daring that has 
won the admiration of all the other 
troops.

The remount station. has been 


called the show place of the Ayer 


cantonment, the prettiest, the most 


restful, the nattiest part of the big 
niilitary camp. This is the place 
where horsemen, picturesque riders 
from the west, men. of independent { 
yaeans, many of them, enlisted men 
who have been attracted to the caval- 
ry braneh of the army, are — being 
molded into form to “earry on” as a 
unit in Ameriea’s big war machines

MINIATURE AEROPLANE MODEL { 
| 7 v. vy RECEIVED FROM NEW BEDFORD SOLDIER . BAR ASSOUATN 


John J. Bouchard of this city has received a miniature geroplane, a 
faithful reproduction of a type of battle plane now in use, from Toby BE. 
Mendes, a former patron of his barber shop, and now a member of an 
aero squadron on the western front in France. 

The model is about nine inches in length from propeller to rudder, 
With wings 6ne foot long. It is made 6f wood and wire and painted 
yattle gray. 

Plyers abroad spend mutch of their spare time in carving models of 
beir planes, this being encouraged by their Superiors, who appreciate the J 

amount of unconscious study of the machines this sport entails. Judge Frank A, Mill

IN 79TH YEAR 


But With Leather at a Dollar 
a Pound “It Takes All the 


Goodness Out of Shoe 
Fixing.” 


The oldest active shoemaker in New 
Bedford is probably Elijah W,, Diek- 
of 12 Lombard street, who in his 
79th year, still repairs shoes in his 
shop at 321 County street. Mr. Dick- 
son is in the best of health, his grip is 
still strong, and his step elastic. ©And 
he declares that he ean still repair 
shoes “better than any machine." 

He was born in Nottingham, Bng- 
land, and came to this country in 1899. 
He has been a shoemaker for 65 years, 
learning his trade when he was only 
eleven years old He has had a shop 
on Wing or on County street for the 
last 18 years. 

Business, he says, is good, but with 
leather at $1 a pound, “it takes all the 
goodness out of shoe-fixing.’”’ He still 
works six days a week. He used to sew 
ladies’ shoes, 18 stitches to the inch, 
with silk, before there were any ma- 
chines. At that time, paper was never 
used in shoemaking. 

Mr. Diekson, -until last summer, 
wore a long beard, which he had never 
eut He has never shaved in his Hfe, 
and his beard soft and fine as silk, 
and as white as snow.

A surprise party was given Jack 
beauparlant at the home of his par- 
ents, 622 Maxfield. street, Saturday 
evening. This was his first furlough 
since he enlisted last May in the navy. 
He is in the electrical class at the 
Brooklyn navy yard, and returned 
Sunday after a 48 hour furlough. 

Among the presents given him were 
wrist watch, Gillette razor set, playing 
cards with case, money belt, comfort 
kit, flashlight and others,

MR. AND MRS. PERCY BATCHELDER HAVE 
GIVEN TWO SONS IN SERVICE OF COUNTR 


MERTON 


J. 


BATCHELDER. WILLIAM W. BATCHELDER. 


Mr. and Mrs. Percy Batchelder, 55 Dartmouth Street, have given twe 
sons for United States war ervice. The younger of them, Cadet William W- 
Batchelder, has just completed the course in ground training in the School 
of Aeronautics at the Massachusetts Institiite of Technology in Cambridge, 
and is now on his way to San Antonio, where he will G¢ommence flying. 
Cadet Batchelder is studying to qualify for the commission of second lieu- 
tenant in the United States aviation corps. 

His brother, Merton J. Batchelder, enlisted June 
marine corps. He is now stationed at St. Thomas, 
Indies,

Canton and for many*years respected

lits last cruise in 1858 had on board

the Canton’s shipwreck on an island 
in 1853, when 33 nien sailed for 45 
days in open boats under a tropical 
{sun a stanee of 3800 miles.

his watch, when the bark struck a

destroyed by fire August 5. It is built on much the same lines as the old cne, although in certain particulars it 
is more modern and.capable of discharging more coal in the same time, than the old one Among the improve

Numerous friends of Harold A, Wile 
mot of 80 Bay street called at his 
home on Monday evening and pre= 
sented him several useful gifts as he 
has been called into service and left 
for Fort Adams 

During the evening games and a 
musical were enjoyed and after re- 
freshments had been served, those 
present danced with music by Stubb’s 
orchestra. : j By 

Mr. Wilmot is well known in this 
city and has an enviable reputation 
as a musician.

William B. Dollard. 


—-———__-_ 


Wiliam B. Dollard of this city, 
Who enlisted in the United States 
army engineering corps several 
months ago, has been wounded. in 
action, according to word received 
in this city yesterday, The news came 
in a letter from the captain of his 
company 

Mr. Dollard was with the New 
York engineers and took part in the 
Cambrai! advance. He was wounded 
in the hands and his injuries were 
not regarded as dangerous. “He is in 
one of the base hospitals “Somewherg 
in France,” 

Mr. Dollard was made a first ely 
private after his arrival in Fra 
He was formerly a.proof-reader ey 
Standard

brother.

23 NCOVERED “s; STORM UNCOVERS 
AN ANCIENT LANDMARK JUST U HIDDEN BUILDING 


Purchase Street Structure, 
Used as Storage, Comes 
to View. 


Served as Backstop to the’ 
Billboard for 


Years. 


When the recent storm blew down 
the billboard on the east side of Pur- 
chase street, just south of North 
Street, it disclosed to view 8 small 
building which had béen hiddeh from 
the gaze of pedestrians for many 
years. Now, the pedestrians who! 
passed the place aré asking of store-} 
keepers in the vicinity: “When was 
that old building moved on to that 
spot?” or “Where did that little 
building come from?” 

The fact is that the building has 
served as a backstop for the biliboard 
for years, Originally the building! 
was used as a photographer's studio 
and during recent years, hidden be- 
hind the board, it has sefved as a 
storage place for the owner. The 
observant citizen, who saw a door in’ 
the board, wondered for what pur- 
pose it was used. It served as an 
entrance to the building, connectin 
with the door on the building itself. | 
lt jis proposed to hide the ldii

Thomas W. Raansden. 


and A. M. of New Bedford, ane one 
of the. most ‘widely. kn@wk  nyusicians 


in this part of the state, died sndien- 
ly. thie. morning at 7 oelsok at ig 
home at 8 Plymeuth street. Because 
ot a slight cold Mr. Ramsden did not 
fill his place at the’ lodge organ last 
evening, but remained home and went 
to bea early. When Mrs. Ramsden 
went to hig room“at 7 o’elock this 
morning she found him breathing very 
heavily and apparently sinking rapid-' 
ly. She summoned Dr. David D. Pratt | 
inumediately, but Mr. Ramisden died § 


before his arrival. 
Mr. Ramsden first ecarplaimed, “Ot 
feeling ill while playing at the Fall 
River church Sunday, beleving then 
that he was suffering from tonsilitis: 
He continued his playing throughout 
both services, however. Last) night 
he did not consider his condition at 
all serious, and beth he and his wife 
felt that a good night’s rest might 
bring him back .to. normal health. > 
Mr: Ramsden was- 40 Years of age 
and had been a resident of New, beds 
ford ever since he was five years old! 
hie was the son of vohn and Jane 
Ramsden and was born in Lensdate, 
R. 1 At an early age; Mr. Ramsden 
began his musical training, taking up 
study of the piano under vcsiah atom, 
fi. Winifred Goff and Miss-H. EB. Hi. 
Forbes, and the organ under Allen W. 
Swan,. of this city and W. A. Locke 
and W. Spalding of Boston, In order 
that he might become more familiar 
with choir teaching, he taéok a special 
course in “boy choir” traming under 
G. E. Stubbs of New York and as @ 
honeymoon trip, took his bride t@ 
England, where for three months theyg 
visited cathedrals and» studied ‘thi 
methods of organists and choirmas® 
ters. This 1ining fitted him for the 
splendid service he performed later 
in New Bedford and Fall River 
churches 
Mr. Ramsden was connected with 
St. Jatnes Episcopal parish nearly 2¥ 
years, singing in the choir as a boy 
under Miss Forbes and succeeding 
her in 1895. After being organist for 
two years, Mr, Ramsden secured the 
position of organist at the Trinitarian 
church, staying, there less than a year 
and left to become choir master and 
organist of ‘St. Tames. 
During this time, Mr. Ramsden was 
the organist of the Unitarian chureng 
Wa aven covering nearly three 
years up to the time when their ser 
vices was changed from afternoon to 
morning He alse had charge of the 
sxrus choir and organ in the Con- 
grezgational church, Marion, for two 
sons and filled the position of pian= 
ist at the Sippican Casino, Marion, for 
seven years. Mr. Ramsden played 
the wedding muric at the marriage of 
the late Richard Harding Davis ang 
Miss Ceeil Clerk of Chicago in Maris 
on some years ago. Mr. “Ramsden 
a went west to Oklahoma for some 
time. 
Later, Mr. Ramsden became organ= 
ist at the County Street M. BH. church 
and in 1914 resigned his place there 
to again take up the Episcopal church 
work at’the Chureh of the Ascensiony 
Fall River 


Mr. Ramsden was organist at Staz 
in the Bast lodge and was also @ 


lrnember of other branches of the Ma- 
sonic fraternity, the Chapter, Council, 
Commandery and Shrine. 

In 1903 Mr. Ramsden was married 
to Miss Blanche Maud March, daugh- 
ter of James March of North Fair= 
haven. He is survived by a widow 
and two children, Beatrice and Willis, 
aswell as by three brothers, George, 
Albert and Irving, and by two sisters, 
Edith Ramsden and Mrs. Richard 
Caldwell. The funeral arrangements 
had. not been completed this morning.

WELL KNOWN NEW BEDFORD GIRL WHO IS 
ONE OF THE CITY’S EXPERT AUTO DRIVERS 


MISS YVONNE DESAUTELS. 


Miss Yvonne Desautels, 
of the overseers of the poor, is one of the city’s prominen 
motor car enthusiasts She has just purchased a new Peerless Seda) 
Model 56 and the accompanying photograph shows her at the wheel .o 
the mew car. She is a skilled driver, the result of having operated severa 


different makes of automobile and to make a run of a day or a week {is 


Ho unusual! occurrence for the attractive young woman, who hag man; 
friends in this city. 

Miss Desautels believes that there is no exercise so refreshing or 
Helpful to the health and complexion as automobiling and the advant 
mee of being able to drive one’s own car adds to the interest of riding

Stephen DL. Sadler. 


Stephen L. Sadler, principal tenor 
vocalist of the Holy Cross Glee club 
who will render one of the solos at t 


concert to be given in the Ulympia 
Theatre Sunday afternoon under the 
auspices of the Civilian Relief com- 
mittee of the Red Cross, is a New 
Bedford boy. Possessed of a fine 
voice, his many friends in this city are 
looking forward to hearing him Sun- 
day. He is also the president of the 
Glee club of his college.

SIX-TENEMENT BLOCK ON AOClt SHNET AVENUE VISITED B FIRE YESTPRVAY APTERNOON, 


REMODELLED POTOMSKA MILL BLOCK BUILDING. | 


Man «29,9197: 


| 
The building pictured above has been so remodelled that it has very little of its old appearance when it ' 
was known as one of the Potomska mill blocks. The blocks were. sold some time ago and the owner has built 
on an addition extending to the line of the sidewalk. A store on the first floor and piazzas on the two upper 
floors break up the monotony of the old box-like structure.

THE STEAMER LEXINGTON, as WW 


boston, Jan, 23.—In a special message to the legislature yeste 
afternoon, Governor McCall recommended that the steamer Lexing| 
which has been used by the district police, be sold. He said that the 
with slight changes, might be used for towing coal. The sale of the 
would mean an annual saving to the state of 32),000.

SERVING AS COOK 
William E. Cranshaw Writes 


of Safe Arrival in France. 


William E. Cranshaw, who is serv- 
ing as cook with the 55th, regiment, 
Battery D, has written his wife at 130 
Fair street, announcing his safe ar- 
rival in France with the members of 
the old Fourth Company of this city. 
He spent some time in England pre- 
vious to arriving at his present camp, 
and states that the majority of his 
chums have very unpleasant recollec- 
tions of their rough crossing of the 
English channel. 

Mr. Cranshaw speaks enthusiastical- 
ly of the life in France, and likes the 
country first rate. On their first 
day he was one of four cooks who 
were called upon to prepare break- 
fast for 1645 men, and states that he 
welcomed the rest which followed his 
arduous duties. He sends his regards 
to his many friends in New Bedford, 


Thomas Cook,

WILL BE READY 
IN SHORT TIME 


Work on Tobey Memorial 
Started Last June---Will Be 
One of the Finest in This 
Section of the State. 


The library which is to-be present- 
ed the town of Wareham by Mrs. 
George Oakes Tobey and Mrs. John 
Hall Jones in memory of George Oakes 
Tobey, Jr., is rapidly nearing comple- 
tion. Work was started on the struc, 
ture last June and it is expected that 
fit will be completed within a few 
flmonths. The building will be one of 
the finest library buildings in this 
section of the country. Only the best 
jof materials have been used in its con- 
struction, and the interior has been so 
built as to provide for the use of the 

———=—imost up to date library ‘systems. 
George Oakes Tobey, Jr., was at one 
time a member of the board of select- 
men of Wareham. Z

Philip A. Green. 


Philip A. Green, treasurer of the 
William C. Jones Company of this city, 
gid the honors as toastmaster at the 
lannual banquet of the American Cot- 
ton Waste Exchange at the Hotel 
Brunswick, Boston, on Tuesday even-! 
jing of last week. With 200 members} 


and guests present, and patriotic sup- 
ort of t’:e nation in the present crisis 
the keynote of the post-prandial 
Speeches, the gathering was most in- 
spiring. 

Not the least of the events of the 
evening was the contribution of $400. 
tor the purchase of tobacco and cigar- 
@ttes for the boys in France, and the, 
vote to send a message to the secre-; 
tary of war asking that tobacco be, 
i ade a part of the ration of the! 
American soldier. 
' The speakers of the evening includ- { 
-@ Ex-Governor David I. Walsh, Jo- } 
}aph Conry, Russian consul, Ex-Mayor 

ohn F. Fitzgerald and three young 

en who have seen service in the war 
Europe, Sergeant H. E. Feeney, | 
rigadier Charles B. Flammond and 
argeant Ashland. The banquet room 
r ofusely decorated. with Ameri-

boys who are now parlez-vousing in France and getting ready to have 
whack at the Fritzies, if. they have not already gone into action. They 
Everett Bradley at the left with his friends, Mills (centre) and Ha 
Connolly (right). ‘They were not particularly husky lads when they 
these parts, but judging from the picture thye are deriving a great de@ 
benefit from their experinece. Bradley is the son of Mr. and Mrs, Tho 
H. Bradley, formerly of Fairhaven, but now of 235 Pleasant street, 
city. He writes that he is in the best of health and looking forward 
happy retinion when he returns,

Sergeant Alfred F. Nye of Fair- 
haven, who was recently one of four 
men picked from Co. F of the 302d in- 
entry at Camp Devens to attend the 
ficers’ training school, has won rapid 
romotion since first going to Camp 

svens. He had been at the camp less 

an two weeks when he was made a 

rporal, and shortly after that time 


aS promcted to the rank of sergeant.’ 


rgeant Nye is_a graduate of the 
husetts Institute of Technology.

have since sailed for the fields of 
France is Constantino Karalekas of 
559 Coggeshall street. Karalekas was 
drafted last September and went with 
the first group to Camp Devens. From 

: here he was sent to a camp in South 
Sarolina, from where he was recently

CAPTAIN THOS. H. 
WILSON DEAD 


Old-time Whaling Master an 


Member of Police Force in 
This City for a Great Many 


Years. 


Captain Thomas H. Wilson, one of 
the old time whaling masters and for 
many years a member of the New 
Bedford police department, died at 
his residence yesterday corner of Sum- 
mer and Middle streets after a short 
illness aged $1 years. 

Captain Wilson in his younger days 
took to the sea, and like all the young 
men of the age went whaling. He 
rose gradually through the grades and 
in the early '60’s med commiand 
He was roaster of shi ps Milton, James 
Arnold and several first class whalers 
of the day, retiring in the early ‘80's 
from the sea. He was apnointed a 
member of the New Bedford police 
department as patrolman on March 
7,,1885, and day housekeeper at the 
Willis street police station on June 


Captai ah Ww in aL) ee 


% 97. qu unassuming man, 
aptain Wile mm was very entertaining | 


when « ngaged ina quite conversation, 


ede had a fund of reminiscences about 
the old whaling days in the Pacific}! 
ocean He leaves a widow, and two! 
children, Mrs. C. E. Carroll and Wal- 
lace B. Wilson. 


BIG STEAMER 
DOCKS HERE 


The Mohonk Is Driven by 
Huge Oil Burning Inter- 
nal Combustion Engines--- 
Brings Coal to Pocohontas. 


The second of the fleet of big oil 
burning steamers: driven by, internal 
combustion engines, to reach this port, 
came in late yesterday afternoon, and 
has been docked at. the Poeohontas 
wharf. It is the Mohonk,/ and she 8 
sister ship to’ the lake Oneida, which 
was recently in port here. Within the 
past three months, New Bedford has 
received more big steamers than at 
fany other time for a great many .ears, 
and their constant arrival and de- 
parture has produced shipping activity 
along the waterfront. 3

WAR CROSS FOR - 
FR. BOUCHER 


Went “Over the Top” with 
the Boys in Patrol 
Fight. 


Former Curate at St. An 
thony’s First New Bedford 
Man to Be Decorated. 


For distinguished bravery 

Man's Land, Rev. Osilas Boucher 
chaplain, a former curate at St. An- 
thony’s church in this city, has been 
decorated with the War Cross by the 
French government, news of this dis- 
tinction being received here today im 
cable dispatches from General Peér- 
Bhing. What the nature of his brave 
acts may have been is not definitely 
known here as the last letter received 
from Father Boucher was dated over 
eight weeks ago. But it is assumed 
as hes tated then that he was. con- 
etantly In the front line trenches; that 
the priest ‘‘went over the top” with 
the boys in a patrol fight and admin- 
Istered comfort and aid to the wound- 
ed. 


Rey. Osias Boucher

machine which Sergeant Frank L.| 
Baylies of New Bedford has,

official credit for it. His ‘second! 
was dropped about three weeks| 
ago, and since then he has been!

risen to the highest rank which 
1e can attain in the French army 
for no flier can hold a commission 
| unless he is a citizen of France

}deavoring to secure a transfer t: 
the American army, but apparent 
| ly has not been successful in this 
| as yet. 
| The place where Sergeant Bay- 
lies scored his victory of yester- 
day is on the French front, re- 
moved about 100 miles from the 
;scene of the German drive. -No 
| details of the fight are given, but 
Baylies for some time has had one 
| of the small fighting planes and 
his business is to pursue and at- 
|tack any German machinés he 
jean find. Dispatches from the 
|front during the past two days 
have told of unusual air activity 
on both sides, 

German machines especially 
|have been reported on scouting 
expeditions over the Allied lines. 
For a French aviator to get credit 
for ‘a victory, he must drop the

hasi lines.

NEW BEDFORD COLORED BOYS BACK 


| 
3/9_)9/ HOME TODAY ON THREE-DAY FURLOUGH | 


Upper Left, Claude M. Ellis; Upper Right, Henry P. Onley;* Lower Left, 
Raymond Barboza; Lower Right, John A, Onley, 


Four of the New Bedford colored boys who went from division two of 
this city to Camp Devens a fortnight or so ago were back home today on 
a three days’ furlough, and will be in attendance tomorrow at the funeral 
services of Private Otis Guinn, one of their comrades. who died “at St 
Luke’s hospital this week of pneumonia, 

The New Bedford boys look to be in fine condition and appear quite 
soldierly in their new uniforms.

tice of her appointment as clerk and 
typist in the office of the surgeon 
,Seneral of the war department, Wash-=

for Washington next week. She is @ 
} graduate of Knowlton school and also 
}of Benton's Business school. Mise 
; Collet has been employed in the city 
clerk's office and in the city treas= 
}urer’s office, although she has never

Southern Mammy to Teach 
New Bedford Women How 
to Conserve Wheat Flour 
and Find Substitutes. Sark 

Jo a : 


Portia Smilfy, ai real Seuthern 
Mammy, is coming to New Bedford 
mext Friday afternoon to teach the 
women of the city how to make the 
delicious corn breads and other deli- 
eacies Which make such fine substi- 
gues for white breads and cakes 
Portia. is coming here at the invita- 
tion of the domesticyeconomy com- 
mittee and the civic federation Mrs 
Andrew G. Pierce, Jr., and Dr. Anna 
W. Croacher are in charge. She has 
Spent some time in many of the 
larger cities of the country teaching 
Women the secrets of her cvuekery in 
Order tO so tempt appetities of their 
families that it will ba a pleasuré 
Father than a sicrifice to conserve 
Wheat flour. Portai has received in 
addition to a great deai of experience 
Gained in her southern home, a short 
course of instruction at the Pratt in- 
Stitute in New York She will be at 
the Star Store at two fifteen. Those 
who have already seen her at wi 
Say it is a great pleasure to watch her. 
She prepares her ingredients with no 
apparent effort and her cenial person- 
ality radiates Ss and she sprinkles 
bright remark ut among the spect 
works

% from Corporal William Bessette of

ing the time which has elasped since 


NS =e 


Corporal Bessette enlisted last May 
and the date of writing he has seen an 
unusual amount of travel. This is his 
second period in Texas. 

After his enlistment he was sta- 
tioned at Fort Slocum and from there 
Was transferred to Fort Sam Houston 
and from there he was transferred tu 
Fort Olgethorpe, Ga. He is now back 
in Texas and is happy as he is fond 
of Texas He writes an interesting 
letter all of which follows 

‘Just a few lines from a New Bed- 
ford boy who le ere last May. I 
enlisted on May of last year and I 
have done quite a lot of travelling 
sinte the This is my second trip to 
Texas. I was sent to Texas last May 
from Fort Slocum I went-to Fort 
San Houston, left Fort San Houston 
for Fort Oglethorpe, Ga., was in the 
11th cavalry in Georgia ard then we 
joined the 22nd cavalry and now they 
turned us into artillery I stayed in 
Georgia for eight months and now 
I am in Texas again, this is a God's 
country toward Georgia. I was just 
reading The Evening Standard of 
March $th and I see that Demers is 
still climbing the ladder of fame. I 
always look for the sporting page 
first 3elieve me it makes a fellow 
feel good to read papers from his 
home town, especially The Standard 
I get The Standard twice a week I 
could not do without it, for it drives 
the blues away. 

“Waco is the best town that I have 
struck yet. There are quite a lot of 
New Bedford boys here in different 
camps and a fellow certainly feels 
good when he meets one. Well, sir, I 
know that this is not a very bright 
letter but I am not much on writing 
and I am sending you one of my pic- 
tures if you care to put it in your 
paper. It is taken with my bunkie, 
Corporal Newman. I am on the right 
in the picture.”

lan-|I had to take another route and

boys are doing splendidly, all are feel-

at the Kaiser. I cannot tell you what 
sector we are in as it is not allowed, 
but it will make all the more to tell 
you about when we come home after 
we get the Kaiser. 

In traveling over the country I saw 
beautiful cities. You would 
never think that you were in a strange 
city. The railroads are the same. The 
cars different and you see a lot 
of soldiers of different nationalities. 
You see German prisoners working at 
all kinds of work. You also see armed 
guards at the bridges and eanals and 
aeroplanes fiying everywhere, especial- 
ly over Paris. There ilways 
or five in the air at or ling 
the city One wo 1 no

shells. Churches were not spared 
they too are in ruins. You could read 
about these devastations all your life 
|}and then not realize how terrible it 
is until you see the scene fpr yourself

WORKSONHIS 
— O4TH BIRTHDAY 


- William Ferguson Up at City 
Barn Enjoying Spring 
. Weather. 


For Great Many Years Was 
Familiar Fisaes as the 


City Messenger. 
1918 


William Ferguson’ New Bedford's 
oldest. city emplove, cele)rated his # 
94th birthday today by working, and? 
when a Standard reporter visited the? 
city barn this morning, he found Mr. | 
Ferguson at his labors. apparently! 

: , 


William Ferguson, 


well and enjoying the spring weather, 
although, of course, a little bent and} 
a little slow of movement. But it} 
might truly be said that his years ace és 
lightly upon him, for he laughed eat: 
chatted and suid that he was entirely | 


able to work, despite his 94 years. f 
Mr. Ferguson makes his home with | 
his daughter on Liberty street, and ~ 
he works from choice, preferring to; 
be out each day and up around the 
eity barn with the other men, where 
there is always “something going on’’ 
to quote his own reason. 

ur, Merguson is best known, per-/ 
haps, for his long years as city mes-- 
senger, having served with variots'} 
mayors. He was first appointed im 
1874, ‘serving throughout that year. 
In 1877 he was again appointed and 
served for three. years. in 1885 he 
was appointed again and then for 
several years in succession was a4 fa- 
miliar figure around the old city hall. 
During the term ‘of the late Morgan 
Rotch he. was not reappointed and 
since then has worked at various! 
trades and at different times has had 
light work with different street gangs. 

Mr, Ferguson has for many years 
been prominent in Union Lodge of 
the Masons and is a deacon of the 
Union Baptist church,

BROTHERS IN SERVICE 


James and Lawrence Maloney 
Like Life in Service. 


Sergeant James H. Maloney, a son of 
Mathias Maloney of 46 Belleville road 
is a member of Company D of the 302d 


saci catncritncdonr nen 


14/8 


$i 


infantry. He attended the officers’ 
training camp at Plattsburg and left 
here for service aS a member of the 
second draft quota from division three. 
Previous to entering the service he 
was employed in the Fairhaven Mill 
| Corporation as paymaster. He was al- 
ways prominent in athletics and is now 
captain of the basketball team of his 
company.

Of the men in the above picture, three are instructors at the Fairhaven barracks. C. B. M. Mathers, who before enlisting was a professor at 
Princeton, is a teacher of_ navigation. Cc. B. M. Winters, whose home is in Marion, teaches seamanship, and C. Q. M, M, Brayton of Fall River, teaches 
semaphore and sigr ling. Cc. M. M. Braley has charge of repair work. C. C. M. Bond had charge of the carpentering, C. M. M, Young the con- 
structing of the 300-foot wharf, and C. M. M. Cunningham the plumbing and heating, in getting the Fairhaven barracks into shape for receiving 


and instructing the hundreds of young men enlisted in the U. S, N. R. F, and>assigned to. this district,

John William Lee of this city is a 
typical case of what Britishers are 
doing in their response to the call 
of the mother country for men and 
more men for the British and Cana- 
dian armies. Mr. Lee has just com- 
pleted the trip from Hingland in or- 
der to enlist his services with the 


cause of the Canadian army forces, 
arriving in this city last week after 
paying a visit to England for four 
months, His observations on the 


other side convinced him that his 
place at the present time was with 
the army, but he decided to return 
to this city to talk the question over 
with his family before he threw in 
his: lot with the fighting forces over 
in France. 

Mr. Lee is a married man with one 
child, and last November paid a trip 
to England to visit several relatives 
in Ashton-under-Lyne and _ other 
towns in Lancashire. He returned on 
the Cunard lIner Orduna, leaving the 
other side oh February 26 and ar- 
riving safely in this country 10 days 
later. For four days the vessel on 
which he was traveling was convoyed 
by British cruisers and war vessels 
through the submarine zone, and was 
one of four on its way to this side.

FR. MILVAINE 
WAS DROWNED 


Chaplain. Met His Death in 


Destruction of Hos- 
pital Ship. 


Priest Since Outbreak of the 
War Had Consecrated Life 


to Service of Country. 


A cablegram received by Mrs, 
Charles J. Hatton of Wilbur avenue, 
North Dartmouth, today from the war 
office in London, announced the death 
on Feb, 26 of her son, Captain Rev. J. 
Mcllvaine, chaplain, who was on the 
hospital ship Glenart Castle, sunk by 
a.U-boat. The cable was as follows: 


“Deeply regret to inform you 
Captain Rey. J. McIlvaine, chap- 
lain to forces, reported missing, 
believed drowned Feb. 2%, in the 
loss of S. S. Glenart Castle. The 
army council express their sym- 
pathy.” 

This laconic message marked the 
end of a priest's life which since the 
outbreak of the war had been consa- 
crated to the service of his country. 
Captain Mellvaine since November, 
1916, had been in the English army, 
had brought comfort to the men in 
the very front line trenches, had been 
gassed and invalided home to a hos- 
pital, and then partially recovering, 
although suffering still from a weak-- 
ness of the heart, had returned to 
duty as chaplain aboard the hospita} 
ship which was taking wounded Aus- 
tralians ‘back to Australia. 

Father McIlvaine was formerly ree- 
tor of St. Andrew's R. C. cathedral 
at Glasgow, Scotland. He volunteer- 
ed as a chaplain and was assigned to 
duty in November, 1916, He was on 
active duty in France for about nine 
months when at Ypres on September 
29, 1917, he was caught in a gas at 
tack, He was taken back to a hos 
pital, and after recovering Was .as- 
signed to the Dublin Fusiliers at Al- 
dershot, England. 

He is survived bya mother, Mrs: 
Charles J. Hatton, a sister, Miss EB 
Melivaine and a brother William T, Me 
Iivaine all of. North Dartmouth.

Alphonse Foisy Is 
Given Farewell 


by His Friends | 


Alphonse Foisy, Jr.,’ [1, I 


Alphonse Foisy, Jr., a member of 
the 16th regiment United States coast 


artillery corps, stationed at James- 
town, R. I., was given a farewell party 
at the home of his parents at 325 


North Front street Thursday night 
during a brief furlough spent in this 
city. He was made the recipient of 
many remembrances from his friends 
here, Miss Bleanor Foisy nas sis- 
ter, making the presentation on be- 
half of the company. A pleasant 
evening was spent in an Ir:promptu 
program of games and music Among 
those present were Mr. and Mrs, Al- 
phonse Foisy, Mr. and Mrs Joseph 
England, Mr. and Mrs. James Billing-

phonse Foisy, Private Adlard LaBrode

|| New Bedford Girl 
| | Has Won Honors By 
| At Brown University | 


| 
Miss Mary E. Carroll. 
' 

Besides being awarded department- 
al honors for mathematics at Brown 
University, Miss Mary E. Carroll of this 
city has been chosen business man- 
ager of The Sepiad, the college paper, 
and vice president of her class. Miss 
Carroll was graduated from the New 
3edford High school with the elass 
of 1915. 


BATTERY D BOYS 
| WERE LUCKY 


Private John J. Coughlin 
Writes His Mother in This 


City---Speaks in Terms of 
Praise of Officers. 


__ tas § 1970. 


John James Coughlin, who is sery- 
ing in France with the Battery boys, 
writes to his mother, Mrs. Catherine 
Coughlin, 270 Austin street, under date 
of Feb. 17. Hes 

Am writing you a line to let you 
know I am well, as it is three weeks 
since my last letter. I suppose you 
have heard that our battery is at the 
front and believe me one is kept on 
the jump. 

Everybody is happy and well, Jim 
Kelly has been up there about a week 
having a crack at them; 1 am about 
three miles in back or him, but I hear 
from him every day and he says he 
is having a great time. so please tell 
Mother Kelly. You would no doubt 
find it hard to recognize me in a hel- 
met.and gas mask, which we are 
compelled to carry all the time. We 
have a fine lot of officers and they 
take as much pride In us as a father 
would in his own son. Every time 
there is any danger they are right on 
the job to tell us what to do. 

I thought when I heard a thunder- 
storm at home it was a lot of noise 
but it is as nothing in comparison to 
the sound of the guns. It is an aw- 
ful sight to see some of the places 
our regiment has passed through, fam- 
ilies having been driven out of their 
homes, which have been blown to the 
ground. It is a common sight to see 
old women digging the ground, but it 
is apparently necessary here owing to 
the men being at the front or in goy- 
ernment employ. I have many things 
to tell you when I get back which 
are not allowed to be said in a letter, 
but which will convince you that our 
jbattery has been very fortunate. 

{ John J. Coughlin.

Oscar Bellenoit, 


Feb. 9, 1918 
Dear Ma: 

Well, I have some very good news 
for you this time I have made good 
during my course ‘and I have been 
kept here for the next course as as- 
sistant instructor. I feel proud of 
}myself as I am the only one kept over 
from this last course out of my whole 
|} regiment It is quite an honor for 
}you and you no doubt will feel proud 
of me as I have put in the best there 
was in me to make good. Am to 
stay here for an indefinite period. 

I am not going to stay here, how- 
ever, all my life. I'm going to keep 
on working hard to see if I can make 
better, There is no doubt something 
better still for me if I make good as 
| instructor, so I ask you—all to pray 
| hard for me as no doubt your prayers 
}were answered this time as mine were. 
| I am leaving for somewhere on the 
| British front for the next week and 
| then coming back to take up my class 
jin signal and map work. 
| You do not need worry over me any 
more as I am able to take care of 
myself very well. 

You can tell my friends.of my good 
fortune as I will not be able to write 
to all of them, 

I haven't received any news of any 
of you for a month now, but, I have 
notified the battery to forward all my 
mail here at the First Corps School 
of Artillery as I will get it in a week 
or so, that is when I come back from 
the British school. 

You cannot imagine what a won- 
derful experience this war is in a 
man’s life. It certainly is splendid to 
see how things go on, 

Well, 1 will write you more of my 
experience here and at the British 
school in my next letter, so take good 
care of yourself, mother, and some 
day I will surprise you. 

By the way. I have found my bag 
that I had lost and I have a few things 
I want to send home in a few days. 

Here is love to all of you. 

From 

. Oscar. 

Use same address for the present 
as they know just where I am at. 

Censored. 

H. R. Miller, 
2Lt. 3 Cav.

Feh », Harold F.. Openshaw. 
4. 


The two sons of Mr. and Mrs. Jokn 
Openshaw of 16 Bolton road, Willia 
=., aged 22 years, and Harold FF 
aged 20, are both in the naval ser- 
vice of the United States, the former 
as a second. class cook of the United 
; States naval reserve force at the New- 
| port base, and the latter as chief petty 
| officer of an army transport now in 
France. 

Harold was the first to enlist, join- 
ing the regular nayy early in April of 
1917 in this city. He was stationed 
for a while at Newport, where he rose 
to his present rank, and later was 
transferred to the base at Portsmouth) 
N. H., where he acted as drill master, 
He was finally put aboard the ship-of 
war Texas, but was-recently trans= 
ferred-to transport duty. 

William went into active service at 
Newport, August 11. 

Both boys, while in this city, were 
conductors for the Union Street Rail- 
way company,

boys who have made 
for 
selves as master marksmen is Private 
Joseph De Terra of 1 Smith court, 
who went to Camp Devens with the 
first draft contingent in div yn two 
from this city. At target practice, 
De Terra hit the bull’s eye squarely 
five tires out cf five shots, a perfect 
score. As far as is known, his rec- 
ord has not been duplicated yet. In 
target practice since then, De Terra 
had made a number of excellent scores 
but has not equalled his own rcord 
since. He never did any shooting 
whatever before entering the army.

Mr, Peters came to the White 
church in June, 1909, succeeding Rev. 
James McAllister, D. D. He was 
culled by the North Christian congre- 
gation from ‘Coshocton, Ohio, where 
he had held a successful pastorate and 
at one time he was pastor of the First 
Christian church of Fall River. 

Since he took the local pulpit, the 
church has expanded considerably, 
having added new members steadily. 
Impetus was given to the growth of 
the church by its consolidation with 
the Middle street and Spruce street 
churches, Mr. Peters is known as one 
ef the most successful ministers of the 
eity and his personality as well as his 


Speaking ability has given him hosts 
h } of friends.

Rev. George Crocker Gibbs 
in France on War Work 


Rev. George Crocker Gibbs, a for- 
mer resident of New Bedford, arrived 
in Franee this week and taken 
of the Technology of 
the American University Union in 
Kurope. Rev Mr. Gibbs will be 
stationed at the parish headquarters 
of the bureau and will engage actively 
in the work of caring for American 
university men,-especially those of the 
Massachusetts Institute of Technology 
who are fighting with the Allies. 

His appointment to this. important 
position will be a pleasant surprise 
for his many friends in this city and 
especially for his mother, Mrs. Lot H. 
Gibbs of Lincoln street, Rev. Mr. Gibbs 
was born in this city in 1378 and af- 
ter attending schools her 
M. I. T. in 1896 During I 
years which followed his graduation 
he was in the employ of some of the 
most widely known engineering con- 
cerns in this country and for a while 
engaged in business for himself in 
New Bedford. In 1908 he abandoned 
engineering and entered the Episcopal 
Theological school at Cambridge. 
Since his graduation from that insti- 
tute in 1911 he has been engaged in 
church work in Oklahoma and more 
recently in a New York city parish. 


has 


bureau 


Privates Kelley and Flanagan. 


M. J. Kelley. 

A New Bedford lad who is serving 
as a wagoner in France is M. J. Kelley 
of 208 Davis street, this city. He is 
with Company D, Ist U. S. Engineers. 
He writes as follows to his parents in 
this city: 


I write you this letter hoping 
reaches you safely and that all the 
good things you have sent have ar- 
rived safely. I have the sweater and 
the socks on. It was certainly some 
outfit. 

Belleve me that old feather bed will 
feel good when I can get in it again, 
We sleep in barns and under 
wagons and some times in houses. 
}present I am in a millionaire’s house 
with the log blazing merrily in the 
fireplace, 
| The sun is shining through the win- 
|dows for the first time in five months, 
jand the boys are enjoying their second 


| bath in two months. 
} 


it 


piece and cigars are about flying. 

I have been up to the front and it 
is hot stuff. I was in the thick of it 
for 36 days, and say gas is a good 
{thing to keep away from. 


| The Salvation Army is doing a good 
work over here. 


Private John 8S. Costa, Jr. 


News of several former New Bed- 
ford men who are now in‘ France 
}conveyed in a letter written to Mayor 
| Ashley by Private John S, Costa, Jr., 
a member of the motor transporta- 
tion detachment C. A. C. A. E, F. No. 
2. Costa, who was one of the gar- 
rison at Fort Rodman, appeals for 
old newspapers and magazines, there 
apparently being a shortage of read- 
ing material among his companions. 
His letter follows: 


“Just a line to let you know’ that 


ad we New Bedford boys are well and} 
+ safe, I 


just got through talking to 
Victor Bolduc who wrote to 
short while ago and I read the piece 
you sent him from the Evening 
Standard. I certainly was glad to 
read a piece from our New Bedford 
paper and would you be kind enough 
to see if anyone has some of the old 
editions of papers and magazines that 
they have discarded and have 
send them to me and I will pass them 
to the boys. 

“There is a lot of boys here from 
New Bedford. I soldiered at 
|Rodman four years or a little over 
;and was put on the reserves. I used 
to work for Jack Haran (Hiram 


you a } 


them | 


Fort 


the : 
At 


Gum costs one franc for a five cent | 


is | 


Wheaton & Sons) as a driver on one | 


of his trucks, 

“I write to my wife and parents in 
New Bedford twice a week but 
have been here only a short while 
and have not got any mail yet. We 
have got a lot of German prisoners 
here and get a lot every day. 

“Recently I met Sergeant Harris 
who used to be my first sergeant at 
Fort Rodman, also Sergeant Doyle 
and lots of others, too numerous to 
mention. I am now writing this let- 
ter with a pencil that came from the 
Continental Wood Screw company, 
New Bedford. It is hard to get ink 
around here. 
and beer here but they advise us not 
to drink it on account of the way it 
puts a man, The French soldiers 
drink a lot of it. 

“Everything is green here and 
planted. Airplanes are flying over- 
head all the time.”

William Beaumont, 

Some of the sensations that the boys 
of Battery D felt when they reached 
front described a letter 
by William Beaumont to his 


mother in this city: 


the are in 


written 


I suppose that by this time you will 
know that we are in the trenches. It 
is different than Lever expected, I am 
;now writing down in a dug out. It 
jhas been very quiet the past few days. 
We have fired a few shots and so have 
the Germans. I had to laugh yester- 
;day, the Frenchmen were having a 
football game and the Germans must 
have seen them for they started shell- 
ing them. You ought to have seen 
them scatter. We all laughed at 
them. 


fine, the best since I joinéd the 
You know all the battery does 
It feels 


ing 
army. 
inot go to the front at once, 
quite funny living: 
it is pretty safe. I had my first les- 
son in being under shell fire Saturday, 
lwe were going up to the front and 
had got to a small village when all 
at once we were shelled by a German 
battery. We were at a halt when it 
happened and the order came through 
to run at a gallop. So I got down 
there to take off the brakes and the 
drivers started the horses. So I was 
left alone and they certainly did go. 
I started running and believe me I 
certainly pounded that old earth. I 
never went so fast in my life, and I 
was in heavy marching order too. But 
any way I got out of the range of the 
fire and sat down and watched the 
shells explode, The shells never bother 
me now. They go whistling over our 
heads and we always look to see 
It is a very 
exciting life and yet it is dreary. for 
you are always under suspense, be-

THE SUNDAY STANDARD SOLD 
Little Personal Stories of New Bedford Boys At the Frort Or In Training Camps As 
In Letters Sent Home \This Past Week.

t 
} 


Henry Adams, 


Private Ellsworth Rogers. 

Another glimpse into the life of 
Battery D is given in Private Rogers’ 
letter: 

Just a few lines to let you know that 
I am well and hope these few lines 
will find you the same. How is every- 
thing down that way? Haven't heard 
from you for about two months. Am 
seeing some of the great sights of the 
—-at the present time. Of course where 
I we are there are no Civilians, nothing 
but soldiers and Americans There 
fisn't much doing here just now so Tf 
thought I would scratch you a line to 
let you Know that I am still among 
tthe living. Of course when we go to 
bed awe don't know whether we are 
going .v» Wake up or not. We have to 
sleep with our gas masks already to 
put on in a minutes notice. Take it 
on an average this isn’t a very bad 
war after all. Of course the big guns 
do a lot of hollering and when the air- 


thing is pretty quiet. The guns are 
roaring tonight somewhere along the 
front. Well I can’t think of anything 
else to Say at this writing, except to 
say that I am waiting to hear from 
jyoeu im every mail, I suppose I will 


drive, and I hope and pray 
that it will be the last battle of the 
war. And that we will march into 
3erlin with the stars and stripes un- 
furled to the breeze. 
Ellsworth B. Rogers, 

1st C. Priv. Co: M:

Joseph P. Collins. 


Collins Brothers, 

Both sons of Mrs. P. Collins of 336 
Davis street, Alfred and Joseph P, 
Collins, are in the military service of 
the United the 
former as member of Battery D, 


102nd regiment United States field 
artillery corps, 26th division, 5ist 
brigade, and the latter of Troop H, 
third regiment United States cavalry, 
of the American expeditionary forces 
to the western war front. Both write 
that they are contented and thriving 
on their treatment in the army.

Private Albert Sherman. 
From Fort Adams comes this chatty 
letter from Private Albert Sherman. 
A reception and banquet was given 
in the mess hall of the Ninth Com- 
wee C. A. C., N. B., at Fort Adams, in 
honor of Captain Mitchell and the 
officers and men who are going to 
jleave the fort soon. The banquet was 
held at 6 30 p. m. with a fine spread 
that more than pleased the boys. The 
|menu consisted of celery, olives, 
pickles, roast turkey, dressing, French 
peas, mashed potatoes, mashed tur- 
nips, giblet gravey, cranberry sauce, 
apple, mince pies, cakes, oranges, ap- 
ples, cigars, cigarettes, ginger ale, and 
{ice cream. The supper was followed 
by speeches from the commissioned 
officers who did not fail to praise and 
encourage the boys for their excellent 
work which has been shown by all 
members of the company. After the 
speeches were made the members of 
ithe company gave an entertainment 
which was enjoyed by all present. The 
9th company quartet sang “Ching 
'Chong Chow” and “The Whole World 
|Is Calling You.” Private Garahan 
with his novelty and sketches 
made a ‘real hit and caused many 
hearty laughs. Private Govermont 
jalso did his bit as vocal soloist. The 
|} 9th company orchestra made the hall 
popular marches’ of the 
David ended the en- 
| tertainment by playing the Star 
}Spangled Banner. The following men 
jare all formerly from New Bedford: 
Major Edgecombe, Captain Mitchell, 
Lieutenant Bhahana, Lieutenant Cox, 
jofficers or former officers of the com- 
pany not of New Bedford. Lieutenant 
Rohbrough, Lieutenant Anderson, 
| Lieutenant Roy, First Sergeant 
|Grosse, Providence, Corporal Hamil- 
iton, Providence, officers and former 
| niders of the company. Privates

Private Sol Cohen. 


Private Cohen is at the front with |} 
the Battery D boys and writes as fol- | 
lows of his experiences: / 

“Tam now at the front and will not / 
be able to write you so often as the| 
Germans are keeping us busy. We 
got our first surprise from the Ger- 
mans on our way to the front. We 
were riding along peacefully and 
looking at the fine country places 
|; when all of a sudden a shell exploded 
110 feet away. Maybe we did not let 
}our horses out and we were soon far 
laway. We are having plenty of ex- 
citement at times and at other times 
it is pretty dull. Only yesterday I 
saw an observation balloon’ shot 
down; then the attacking aeroplane 
; was sent down, But we are getting 
used to the sound of bursting shells 
and aeroplanes. But do nt worry as 
we will.be home before long. 

I am feeling fine and in good 
health. I am getting stout and am 
raising @ moustache so you will not 
know me when you see me. 

I am glad that I am over 
cause it is something awful the way 
the Germans have treated the 
French. Have seen a few villages and 
they are all in ruins, nothing left but 
the old people. The young people 
were either killed or carried away. 
What they could not carry off they 
blew to pieces. I do not wonder that 


here be- 


the whole world is against them and ’ 
|i hope to get a few Germans myself.” }

|

| 


| with. 
;see them once more before they go

William A. Bulcao, 
Conditions “over there” are clearly 
pictured in the following letter from 
William A. Bulcao of the medical de- 
partment of the 14th Engineers. 
Just a few lines to let you know 


that I am still alive but not as well as 
I would like to be. The weather has 
been fairly good lately but, previous 
to that it was very cold and damp. It 
snowed quite a little here but I am 
afraid not quite as much as in the 
states. Although the thermometer 
does not register as low as in the 
|States I believe it is just as cold if 
jnot colder owing t» the dampness in 
jthe air. When it rains, one can find 
nothing but mud. In some Places it 
is exceptionally deep. 

The old shack just rocked and 
shook from the concussion of the shell, 
At times the shock was greater than 


) 
i 
t 
j 


| 


}others owing to the size of the shells @ 


fhe was dropping. 
ja lot of noise when he gets loose. We 
are ready for him now and,I guess he 
won't get loose for another good long 
while. 

I will be going on furlough very 
soon but not to the states. I wish 1g 
was going, it’s the only place in all 
the world. Few of us realized what 
home was until we got out here.” T 
expect to go to Paris now or England 
later. 

We have to be very careful on what 


}we say in our letters owing to strict 


censorship. I don't think my men- 
tioning the above two places is mili- 
tary information for others are writ- 
ing about going to Paris and England. 
If it is considered military informa- 
tion it will be scratched out, but I 
doubt very much if it is, 

I receive papers from home once in 
a while “The Evening Standard” and 
it seems rather a treat to be able to 
read the home news at this distance. 

T have met already quite a few 
others in this regiment and others 
from New Bedford. We get togethee 
and we tell of the good old times wa 
used to have back home. 

I see that New Bedford is sending 
quite a few men away to be trained 
for the trenches. I wish them the 


i best of luck. I wish I could meet some 


of them. Several names which TI read 
in the paper I am well acquainted 
It would be just the thing to 


into the lines. I suppose there will be 
many I will never see again, 


He sure does make|

| Bombs struck in several places, and | 
there were about 20 killed and many 
injured. We went up again today. to! 
see the place where the bomb had 
struck and it sure ripped things up 
some. It was a very exciting and in- | 
teresting experience, but I hope that 

they don’t keep it up. 
Frank Baylies has brought. down his 
first Bosche and has heen officially 
awarded the Croix de Guerre with a 
palm. I suppose the papers haye 
given you all the particulars. It sure 
is great of him, and he deserves a 
whole lot of credit. I hope that he 
pulls through all right and not have 

any misfortunes.

|the roof and sat there watching the 
bombs, the lights from the air-planes, 
ete, (One can ‘tell the difference in the 
hum of a French plane and a Bosche. 

The French plane has a steady roar, 
while Fritz has little iIntermissions be- 
|tween his explosions). We had no 
|More than got there when—one, two, 
three, four. Bang, Bang, Bang, Bang, 
{t s@emed as though they had hit 
Tight next to us. The sky was illu-! 
|)minated with the flare, and our bulld- 
ing swayed to and fro. We beat it 
just as fast as we could for the cellar 
|for protection. From then on it was 
one continual roar of bombs, anti-air 
craft guns, machine-guns, and the rat- 
ta-tat-tat from the planes in the air. 
‘They dropped gas bombs and Killed 
many, who had taken refuge in a sub- 
way, in this manner. 

Secretary of War Baker was given a 
grand reception on his arrival here. 
The minister of war’s building was 
smashed to smithereens and that 
whole street was a shower of glass. 
The front line trenches have got noth- 
ling on this place now. Our place is 
jonly about four blocks away from the | 
Place de Concorde where all this took 
place, and this is what they always 
make for as it is about the centre of 

ivities. If they keep it up we'll all 

ve to go around with steel-helmets 
and gas-masks. 

Do you get reports promptly “‘‘over 
|there’’ om these raids, etc? There 
lare a buneh of our engineers, and 
ldoughboys, here at the hospital all] 
wounded, and when one sees them it] 
sure makes them realize that we are 
right in it for fair, but we are going 
to come out on top just the same. The 
haven't arrived yet, and am 
still watching the mails eagerly. Es- 
pecially want the one in which you } 
lsay you enclosed photos taken of you 
two out at Long Pond by Dr. Pease. | 
‘Hope that they haven’t gone down on 
the way over. Am busy morning |[ 
land night, and never worked harder in| 
jmy life, Things are being rushed, 
and it means that I have to work) 
lnights very often, so the old cot and 
mess certainly go good, but I don’t 
| mind. Spring has come to stay here 
1 guess, and the days and nights are 
wonderful. The days are warm, and 
in the afternoons the boulevards are 
crowded, as well as the parks, but 
since these last two raids one right 
jJafter the other the streets are ab- 
solutely dark, and very few people 
jare to be seen. What lights there 
are are dark blue, and everything has 
a sombre air. Maybe the Statue of 
Liberty and the good old U. S. A. 
won't leok good to me, and a whole 
lot of others too, I guess. Well when | 
I write again in a day or two maybe| 
i'll have another raid to write to you} 
about, but 8 hope they don’t come 
as close as they did before. 

Remember I love you two better 
than life itself, and if ill should befall 
ime you two will be my last thought. 
|\May God bless you and watch over 
you and me, too, and help us to Join 
when the time permits. With best 
love and wishes to you, and remember 
mé to all the folks, 

Lovingly,

MEETS OLD CHUMS 


Frederick Vogt Sees Battery 
D Boys. 


In a letter to Rev. Henry Reumann, 
astor of St. John’s Lutheran church, | 
as received a letter from Private | 
Frederick Vogt of the 103d M. G. B. | 
in which he tells of meeting some of | 
ie Battery D boys in France. 

\Dear Friend:—Just a few lines to 

you know that I am well and as 
ppy as can be under the circum- 
mnces that we are in just at present. 
as very much surprised to get 
ir letter and you may be sure that 
was more than welcome, I also 
a number of letters at the same 
2 last night and among them was 
tter from home and all the mail 
| I got has made me very cheerful 


annot help but sing now and| porter w 


| that it stays this way. 


thank the good Lord for belng so 
good and kind to me in the days of 
my great need. Perhaps I may get} 
some more mail tomorrow and I am| 
sure that it will be welcome. I met 
seme of the New Bedford boys who| 
belong to Battery D. I also met 
Frank Wunschell among them and I 
was mighty glad to see some of my 
old chums. 

At times I feel rather lonesome and 
homesick. I try to attend church 
services every chance I get but you 
know how soldier's life is, one 
moment here and the next he is on 
his way for somewhere else But I 
am trusting the Lord for I knew long 
before I joined the army that Jesus 
is my very best friend and I know 
that I am one of his children and he 
is taking care of me good 
kind father takes care of his only 
child. Today as I look back to the 
days that are gone I can more 
and more of the kind deeds Jesus has| 
done for me and since I came to} 
France the Lord has taken a firmer 
and better hold on me. He has helped ! 
me to resist temptations and evils and 
I can always feel his hand no matter 
what I do or where I go. Yes, dear 
friend, I can confess that Jesus is my 
Redeemer and Saviour, that I am his 
child, and that he is my father In 
heaven. May his will be done on 
earth as it is in heaven and if is 


a 


as a and 


see 


it 


| his will that says that my body shall 


be buried in France my 
to heaven just the same. But I trust 
that the Lord will carry me through 
all danger and bring me back safe 


soul will go

Somewhere in France, 
Jan. 15, 1918. 

Dear Mother and Family—aA great 
surprise was in store for me today, 
when Mr, Good Fortune favored me 
with two letters and one of my Christ- 
mas boxes, the one with the cake and 
all the good things you so kindly sent 
me. You cannot imagine what a feel- 
ine of contentment a day like this 
gives you, and all the boys look for- 
ward to it. The mail orderly is the 
most harrassed man of the unit, and 
it is funny to see the fellows each in|\’ 
turn asking him if there is any for 
them. Of course he becomes mighty 
sick of having 200 fellows ask him 
daily, and it amuses me to see the 
look of worriment on his face as he 
sees us. 

Our work over here ts really in an} 
embryo state yet, but still considerable 
headway is being made, ‘The hospital 
is running, and up to date we have 
about 250 patients. They are all sick 
| eases or operations now, but if a 
transport goes down the survivors are 
brought into this port, and the ill or 
wounded come to this hospital. Later, 
no doubt, when the spring drive is on 
in good shape we will get the cases} 
sent back from the front, 

Right now the inconveniences are 
too numerous to mention, and for that 
reason the work is hard and compll- 
cated. We have a first class operating 
room and some of the wards are not 
so bad. You see this place was a 
eonvent and has many rooms, which 
|makes it inconvenient for us hospital 
corpsmen. In large rooms we have 
as high as 30 patients, but that is only 
in the main chapel and sort of auxil- 
lary one. Most of the other rooms 
hold from two to ten. When we are 
filled up this building will probably 
hold. seven hundred. The French, 
yho had it before us, put nine hun-

Lawrence F.. Rooney. 

Mr. and Mrs. John P. Rooney of 93 
Bedford street are today in receipt of 
a letter from their son, Lawrence F. 
Rooney, telling of his safe arrival at 
, Kelly Field, San Antonio, Texas, Fri- 
vate Rooney left Camp Dix, Wrights- 
town, N. J. (where he has been sta- 
tioned since he enlisted in December), 


fon Monday the 1ith, arriving at Kelly 
||}Field on Friday the 15th. 


Private 
Rooney writes that the trip south was 
very interesting. They passed 


through Pennsylvania, Ohio, Indiana, 


LTRS, Missouri, Arkansas, and then 
,into Texas, and wherever the train 
istopped for any length of»time en! 
lroute, the boys got out and took aj} 
'short hike for exercise. In Philadel- 
| phia, which was the first stop, the 
train was met by the Red Cross so- 
ciety, who gave the boys cigarettes, 
and apples and stamped post ecards to 
send messages to their friends. In In- 
dianapolis, the people turned out in 
crowds and gave them a warm wel- 
come. At Pine Bluffs, Arkansas, the 
people came to the train and brought 
candy and cigarettes to the boys. On 
the journey, coming south, the letter 
reads, I saw miles and miles of corn- 
fields and cottonfields. The farmers 
,are ploughing now; and in August, 
they tell me, the cotton fields are a 


| great sight, white as snow. I’m an- 


dred in it, but it could hardly be ex- 
pected that Americans could stand 
such a crowding of people together, 
unless it is absolutely necessary, au 
was the case with the French, 

In regards to the duration of the 
war, there is the same sort of specu- 
lation over here among the French 
asin the United States. The more 
conservative French look for two 
more years, while others tell you it 
| can’t last six more months, Person- 
lally I feel that two years is closer 
than six months, but as I say, it is 
mere speculation. 

The supreme 
coming this spring. 
Germans to hit again at Verdun, 
!!pringing enough pressure to bear 
| lalong the line to keep the English 
'lpusy so they cannot send help. 
| America has a substantial army over 
{here, but not substantial enough to 

be much of a factor in an offensive 
| movement. The French have a fine 


effort is certainly 
I look for the 


it takes a lot of undercurrent work. |}xjous to see them. We finally arrived at 


It will take a long while before it 
breaks out in q conflagration. But 
it surely will eventually. 

Personally, I-wish the whole thing 
Was over now. I’m not ready to quit 
by a long shot, but the military life is 
a hard one. Plenty of give and take 
to it with you on the taking end all 
the time. Our officers are a fine lot 
and I like them. Incidentally it is 
not a bed of roses, as the posters 
would have it seem. 

I guess I\haye bored you long 
enough for this time, so will close, 
thanking you for all the deeds you 
are doing for me. They certainly are 
| appreciated. 

As ever, your loving son, 
Bill,

Harry Ashworth, 

A tribute to the late Vernon Castle 
is contained in a letter written to 
Mayor Ashley by Harry Ashworth, a 
New Bedford man who was a mem- 
ber of Captain Castle’s squadron. Ac- 
ecmpanying the letter is a photograph 


of Captain Castle two days before he 
fell to his death. Ashworth, whose 
home is at 325 Earle street, is serv- 


ing in the Royal Flying corps of the. 


British army. He writes as follows: 
“We are now stationed at Camp 
Benbrook, 


work of training air pilots, both 
American and British. We have bee; 
here for the winter and expect to 
back to Canada_

says, “and we are carrying on the}

et monkey on his shoulder, Vernon 
Castle was a captain in my squadron 
land a whiter man never lived. It 
jsurely was hard to lose him, but it is 
all part of the big game.” 


| George G. Davis. 

George G. Davis, the son of Mr. and 
|Mrs. Peter Davis of 54 Peckham 
(street, has been “over the top” five 
jtimes since the outbreak of the pres- 


\ént war as a private of the 38th bat- 
talion, British expeditionary forces to 
jthe western war front. In letters to 
his parents here he tells but little of 
the part he has played in holding the 
firing line against the onrush of the 
Hun or of his harrowing experiences 
in the hell of No Man’s Land, but begs 
|that we who have remained at home 
do all in our power to forward the 
war work of the Young Men’s Chris- 
tian association, without which he de- 
clares it would be impossible for the 
fighting man to bear the terrible hard- 
oe to which he is subjected under

Emile Corderre. 

Emile J. Corderre, formerly of 145 
Deane street and for the past six 
months a private of the 80th regiment 
United States field artillery corps, is 


stationed at Fort McArthur, Waco, 

Tex, He enlisted in this city and after 
i|@ few days’ preliminary training at 
Fort Slocum, New York, was trasfer- 
red to Fort Oglethorpe, Georgia, 
where he remained several weeks be- 
fore being shipped to Fort Arthur. He 
likes army life and is entirely satis- 
||fled with Texas and the treatment he 
(jis accorded there.

Frank L, Cuddy. 
Frank L. Cuddy, whose home tn this 
| city is at 190 French avenue, and who 


jis stationed at Kelly Field, San An- 
tonio, Texas, has written the following 
letter home concerning his experiences 
| in the army: 

Dear Folks: —Well, here I am 

sound and well with no kick about 
parmy, life although everything here 
jis not pie, but we are learning every 
| day. I have been here since Friday 
but this is the first chance that I have 
had to write. 

We had a fine trip across the coun- 
try. We saw miles and miles of coal 
cars loaded that have been side- 

| tracked all winter. We also saw miles 


and miles of corn on the stalks that! Portuguese troops are here, ana Suffering hardsh ps 


be 


picked on account of 


;about but we are not allowed to speak 
}of them, 

| It is getting late now, and as I 
; want to mail this letter tonight I will! 
{close with love. 

We have to go to bed at 9 p. m. 
jand get up at 5 30 a. m. I will write 
later and do not forget to write me. 

Your loving son and brother, 

. Frank 

Chaplain John B. DeValles. 

In a letter written to Dr. C. L.| 
Pitta, Fr. DeValles, chaplain of the! 
104th U. S. infantry, tells of what 


Portugal is doing for the cause of the 
Allies, 
My Dear Dr, Pitta: 


At last I have found a few spare 
moments to pen you a few lines from 
far away France. While in Paris I 
mailed you a couple of postcards! 
which I hope passed the censor. 

Well, Mon Cher, as I already wrote 
}you, your friend Dr, Vianna is a/| 
prince of good fellows and very well | 


| known in the beautiful city of Paris. 

How y6éur ears must have tingled| 
during the hour of my interview with 
him. I promised him that on my 
next furlough in Paris I would be his| 
guest, I am therefore under another! 
obligation to you for getting me ac- 
quainted with such a splendid char- 
acter, 

Though an American chaplain of 
the American Expeditionary Forces I 
could not forget that Portuguese blood 

j flowed in my veins, 

I looked up some officers of the 
Portuguese army in France and made 
several inquiries that perhaps exposed 
me to the suspicion of being a German 
spy. I found that nearly 100,000

ANOTHER NEW BEDFORD MAN IS GIVEN 
COMMISSION IN THE AVIATION SERVICE 


LIEUTENANT AUGUSTUS M. BRENNEKE. 

Augustus M. Brenneke of this city, a second lieutenant in the United 
States aviation corps, is now stationed at Camp Dix, Dallas, Texas, where 
he is attending the officers’ training school. At the outbreak of the war 
Brenneke was a member of the cavalry of the Connecticut national guard, 
but was transferred to the aviation section of the regular army, and after a 
course of instruction at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology was as- 
sicned to duty at the Garden City, N. Y., fields. 
to South San Antonio, Texas, where he |received his commission and ap- 
sointment to the officers’ nool. a

A Valuable Find. 

The tug Westerly and her prize re- 
mained at Merrill’s wharf all day yes- 
terday, Captain Robinson awaiting 
the arrival of his agent from Wester- 
ly.. The captain would make no state- 
ment as to the disposition of the eraft 
and cargo which he had picked up 
and brought to this port as salvage, 
except to say that he expected that! 
a conference between his agent, Cap-| 
tain Schroeder and the owner of the 
thousand bushels of oysters would de- 
termine the percentage that would be 
granted for salvage work, He could 
not say how long the oysters would 
remain fresh, 

The vessel, lying on the south side | 
of the wharf, made quite a sight, and 
many people went to the wharf dur- 
ing the afternoon to see the wreek- 
age. The two big masts and the sails, | 
which were spread when the Bliza 
Jane was struck by another vessel, 
were floating alongside the boat, just 
as they were towed into port from] 
Hen and Chickens. The boat has a] 
thousand bushels of oysters on board, 
worth a dollar a bushel.

PAINTER SWIFT DIES AT ACUSHNET HOME 


CLEMENT SWIFT, | 
PAINTER, DEAD 


“Vuasid bv 


“The Waif,” in Free Public | 
Library, One of Best Ex- | 
amples of His ey ; . 


| Aisabraht Painter Had Thrill- 


in War of 1870. 


Clement Nye Swift, an artist wide- 
ly known for his masterly full size 
reproductions of works of Rosa Bon- 
heur and other great French masters, 
and for his own strongly individual 
and striking paintings, died at his 
home in Acushnet yesterday. He had 
in ill health for some time. 

Mr. Swift's work was widely known 
For years he has not failed to con- 
tribute to the exhibitions of the New 
Bedford Art club some work of ex- 
eellent technique. Examples of 
best work are to be found in the Free 
Publie Library and_in the Acushnet 
Town hall. Pertaps the finest ex- 
ample of his own work at his best is 
the large canvas, ‘The Waif.” The 
one which Mr. Swift presented to his 
home town in 1914 is a fine copy of 
Miss Bonheur’s ‘The Plowhig.”" 


ing Adventures in France | 


been 


his 


'M 


jlation 


His Copy o% Rosa 


The artist was the son of Rudolphus 
Swift and was born at the Head-of- 


He was a pupil at Friends 


academy. His early 
toward animal painting, 
Artist Hinkley of Bos- 
ton for instruction. Mr. Hinkley 
did not desire a pupil, but strongly 
advised going to France, for at that 
time the advantages for preliminary 
instruction—at present so numerous 
and adequate—were almost totally 
wanting. In France he began. copy- 
ing in galleries of the Louvere 
Luxembourg, drawing in the school 
of Beaux Arts, and in the Atelier of 
Adolph Yvon Later he became a 
pupil of the celebrated landscape 
painter, M. Henri Harpighies, and a 
regular yearly exhibitor @® that larg- 
est and best exhibition ii the world, 
the French Salon. 

Mr. Swift gvent to POnt Aven in 
Brittany, and\remained during the 
winter of 1870)ywhile the French and 
Prussian war Wes in. progress, 


the River. 
inclination 
and ap- 


was 
he 


plied to 


Sources of the regi@m, decided to make 
it a headquarters. WHfere he found 
much congenial soci@fy and formed 
one of the group of @Btists, all of 
whom have become Well known, 
among them being Hobert Wylie, H. 
Bolton Jones, Frank Jones, Burr 
Nicholls, Thomas Havenden™and W. 
L. Picknell. It wasfin Brittany that 
he painted all of the Jargest and most 


important pictures exhibited in France| 


and afterward in New York and oth- 
er American exhibitions. Among this 
group is “Wreckers,” representing 
the wreckers lying in wait for a vessel 
which is drifting to the shore, This 
picture when sent to Paris elicited 
by return post a letter of congratu- 
from the masters, 
France in 1870. 

in the spring of 1870 Mr. Swift was 
studying in the Museum of the Lux- 
emburg at Paris under the supervi- 


sion of the French master, Harpig- 
nies, After pursuing his studies for 
some time Mr. Swift became impa- 
tient, went to his master one day, 
said that he would like to do sone- 
hing w h ie, ed his per- 


and 
fonce 


and

3onheur’s “The Plowing.” 


mission to make a copy of Rosa Bon- 
heur’s masterpiece. The master was 
very much surprised at his audacity 
and teld him in a reproving manner 
that he must wait a month; 

This reply, however, did not’ dam- 
pen Mr. Swift's ardor, and at the end 
of the month he asked again, only to 
be greeted with the same reply. Af- 


| ter the second month had passed by 


his enthusiasm was so 
he ordered a canvas, on his own ve-} 
sponsibility, and gave orders to the 
men to place it before the great mas- 
terpiece. After completing all ar- 
rangements he went up to his master 
more, and insisted that hé ba 
allowed to paint the picture. 

The master was so surprised that 
he gave his sanction, In speaking of 
the incident, Mr, Swift said: “If I 
had been a Frenehman and persist- 
ed the way I did, especially the sec 


aroused that) 


;ond time, it would have been consid- 
Jered a serious breach of etiquette, but 


being an American it was very little 
thought of, as I was not supposed to 
know much. 

“When JI first started the 
I was given instructions to draw in 
the picture and to make the lights 
and the shades. After all the pre- 
liminary preparations had been made, 
as I thought, the master who was evi- 
dently not an impressionist, said to 
me in broken English, ‘When IT paints 
ze picture I give nine days to ge 
drawing and one day to ze painting.’ 

Up to that time Mr. Swift’s copy 
was the only one that had been made 
life-size, the dimensions being about 
§ feet by 4 feet. While he was work- 
ing on his painting the Franco-Prus- 
He was 
forced to leave it unfinished and fled 
to Pont-Ayen in Brittany, in the 
northern part of France, which he! 
knew to be a congenial place for ar- 
tists. From this place, moreover, he 
could, if. necessary, easily cross the 
English channel. 


Adventures in 1870.

other artists when he started on the}

Member of U. S. Navy Com- 
pletes Second Trip Across. 


A card with the printed statement: 
“The ship on which I sailed has ar- 
rived safely overseas,” has been re- 
ceived by James Gill of 175 Phillips 
avenue from his son, Harold Gill. The

The new central police station has 
already been found inadequate, for al- 
though it possesses accommodations 
for almost every officer of the force 
no arrangements have been made for 
the headquarters of the most import- 
ant personage of the entire depart- 
ment—Saxon, 

Saxon is the department mascot, find 


Harold Gill. 


arrival of the card marks the sec- 
ond safe trip across the ocean that has 
ended safely for young Gill, who en- 
listed in the United States navy at 
Newport on September 19th, 1917. 
After a short stay at Newport he was 
transferred to the Brooklyn navy yard 
and from there he made his first trip 
across last February. His second 
trip was in April. He had the unique 
experience of seeing a U-boat on the 
first voyage, but the ship upon which 
he was stationed succeeded in avoid- 
ing the torpedo. : 

Gill is 18 years old and made his 
home in this city for 17 years. He 
attended the Lincoln school and after

Robert Haworth, 


But I must not tire you, my dear 
friend, by writing any more. Tell my 
New Bedford friends I am well and 
hope to return when the war is over, 
und that will be soon, I hope. If the 
Boche bullgts get me as I go among 
|\the wounded and dying, well, I will be 
|game and will be glad to have lived 
to do my part for the cause of hu- 
| manity and the religious welfare of 
\the brave men of my regiment. 


Everett Haworth, 
Rats Are Big Fellows.

shoes and other articles all over the

Fairhaven.

: : From Left to Right, Nicholas Perry, James Sher and Alfred J. Morin. 

Anthony Morris. The above picture is that of three New Bedford boys who are in train- 
ing at Kelley Field, San Antonio, Texas, and judging from their appear- 
ance, all three are enjoying soldier life, They are Nicholas Perry, James 
Sher, and Alfred J. Morin. In a letter in this city, they wish to be re- 
membered to all their friends here.

Mr. Sadler returned to New 
ford, where he remained until 
ary, 186+, when he enlisted again in 
the famous Fifty-eighth regiment, 
Company &, under Captain William 
BE. Mason 

The company was sent to Read- 
ville, and went from there to Gallups 
Island in Jsoston harbor. While at 
the latter-place Mr. der was taken 
ill, and rad to be to the hos- 
pital in Boston. covered so 
slowly that on Jul 1864, he was 
discharged for disability and came 
back to New Bedford. 

Mr. Sadler is an old whaleman and 
had sailed to all parts of the world 
on the whaling ships »f New Bedford 
His fifst voyage was made in the 
bark Platina, out cf New Beéfara 
under’ Captain Thomas Lee. They 
cruised around Cape Horn, and across 
the South Pacific” whaling grounds 
and returned in 1853 with a ful] 
eargo. Later he shipnéd again in the 
Champion under Captair Thomas 
Pease. This time they went further 
north into the Okhetsk sea and 
around Kamchatka. On their way 
south again Mr. Sadler had an argu- 
ment with the captain which resulted 
in his leaving the ship at the Sand- 
wich islands, where he re-shipped on| 
the Jeannette of Westport, bound for 
home. Other vesSels which he has 
sailed in are the India, Captain Flan- 
ders, and the Olympia, Captain Ryan.

Armoud Fournier, 


SECOND TRIP ACROSS: 


Armand Fournier Has Excit- 
ing Voyage. 


Mrs. Armand Fournier has just re- 


| ceived news of the safe arrival “over 


there” of her husband, Armand Four- 
nier. This makes his second suc- 
cessful trip across aboard an Amer- 
ican transport. He spent a few days 
with his wife at 43 Calumet street 
about a month ago. After a danger- 
ous and exciting trip, although made 
in safety, and a stay of four months in 
France, he had many experiences to 
relate. He is 21 years old and has 
| served in the navy eleven months, for- 
merly on the U. S. S. South Carolina 
and U.S. S. Ohio.

LETTER TO ELKS 


Fred C, Calnan Says He Likes 
Army Life. 


Fred C. Calnan of this city, who is 
}at Kelly Field, South San Antonio, 
Texas, has written to his brother Wks 


ing the heavens are lit up by the flash 
ef the big guns and star shells which 
last quite a few minutes 

“IT have seen quite a 
Battery D from New Bedford. It 
seemed like home to meet them, tT} 
pee by all the papers that I received 
that it was dreadful cold in the states 
and a lot of snow. The poor people 
must have suffered being cold. 1 
hope the war will be over soon and 
we gan all be home again, but it 
looks now that it will be a long time 
before it ends. One of our lieuten- 
ants was saying the other day that 
we would be going home in the spring 
ef 1920. It is the worst war that I 
was ever in.”

Unexpected Ways. 


When Robert Hayes, a recently en- 
listed New Bedford boy serving with 
the regular army at Fort Slocum, New 
York, was having his hair cut a la 
army the soldier-barber simply wrap- 
ped a small towel about his neck and 
got busy, but Private Hayes objected 
and insisted that a newspaper be lain 
across his knees to prevent the hair 
from falling on his trousers. There 
Was some difficulty in securing a news- 
paper, the only man in the barracks 
boasting possession of refusing 
absolutely to surrender it until he had 
learned to what purpose it was to be 
put and for how long it would be out 
of his hands. 

When at last Hayes gained tempor- 
ary possession of the paper he found 
it to be none other than The Standard, 
and was easily able to realize why the 
man had been so reluctant to give it 
up. Incidently, he understood that this 
man was another from his own home 
town and made a fast friend. 


BLOOMER IN ENGLAND 


Is'Enroute to the European 


Theatre of War. 


William W. Bloomer of this city, a 
private of the 162nd Aero squadron 
of the American expeditionary forces 
to the western front in France, has 
arrived safely in England enroute to 


one 


William W. Bloomer. 


| the theatre of war, according to 
vices received by his mother in this 
He writes that he is in the best 
of health and prepared to undertake 
his share of the task of forcing the 
retirement of the Hun.

' CHAUFFEUR FOR MAJOR { 


Corporal Louis T. Chace Now! 
| Stationed at Washington. 


; Louis T, Chace, formerly a member 
of the old Fourth company and now 
with Co. D of the 56th engineers, is 
now stationed at Washington, D, G 
He has heen promoted to corporal of, 
his company and is acting as chauf- 
feur to the major of the company, He 
left this city for Fort Slocum three # 
weeks ago and was sent from there 
to Washington. He is the son of Mr. a 
and Mrs, N. S. Chace of Padanaram, k
